When there is a lack of reliability, this reduces the opportunities to run a business, to live, work and develop rural well-being. Interruption can take place anywhere and is at all nothing that only affects people in rural areas. On the other hand, there is a greater sensitivity, as data communication is often the only mode of transport that is not affected by distance but, on the contrary, often bridges large distances and makes it possible to operate and interact with markets in the rest of the world despite a large distance. Madam President, I would like It is important for market participants in telephony and broadband to assume their responsibilities also financially in the event of disruptions and disruptions. In the worst case, losing your connection as an entrepreneur means a loss of income, almost regardless of what you do or what you deliver for services. So we have become dependent, and so much more efficient and safer communication we get with reliable deliveries to our customers via broadband networks and telephony. The Center Party believes that it must be legislated to introduce an interruption compensation from broadband and telephony providers to customers. It is not to make up for damage and loss of income, because it is not possible. However, there must be a financial incentive for suppliers for security of supply and for them not to systematically disperse certain customers for the benefit of others. Today, I am in favour of reservation 11. Every day, women use on average 12 different cosmetic products in their bathroom cabinets. There are clear shortcomings in both marketing and legislation, while interest in the issue is perceived as weak from policy and, above all, from the industry. It is totally unacceptable and a flagrant example of what is commonly called greenwashing, to make something seem environmentally friendly, although it is exactly the opposite. This is not the case with organic food. There is already an established labelling system that is perceived and reliable. The Center Party wants to see a common European eco-label system for organic and natural cosmetics that is fair and reasonable, in the way that we have for other mixed-content products with which we come in direct contact. i-d299eb6ffd2079c9-1153	Madam President, I would like I, too, would like to begin by stating that a horse is not the same thing as a refrigerator and that a dog is not the same thing as a purse. i-d299eb6ffd2079c9-1156	I do not know how many different examples of this I could line up here today. This is, among other things, the background to the fact that we in the Alliance in the Committee on Civil Liberties, following the diligent work of a number of colleagues who have also exercised the matter, have pushed through an announcement, which I strongly urge, that the Government, both nationally and within the European Union, should initiate considerations on the rules of the Consumer Sales Law for the purchase of live animals, are properly designed. Madam President, I would like When selling a horse between a trader – most animal breeders are private individuals, who run their own business – and a private person today it is the Consumer Sales Law that governs both the purchase and a possible complaint. An animal is seen according to the Consumer Sales Law as any other commodity. That means, Madam President, that the customer has a right of complaint on horses and dogs, just like any other consumer product. A buyer can thus claim that an animal was already wrong at the time of purchase, and the seller is then fully responsible for proving that the defects did not exist at the time during the first six months. Of course, this relationship is not applicable to live animals, which are constantly changing during, for example, environmental change or new handling or training procedures. The Consumer Sales Law applies to movable property and should primarily concern consumer goods such as televisions, cars and refrigerators, but certainly not live animals. In the preparatory work to the Consumer Sales Law, the scope – which goods would be included – was discussed and the conclusion was that it would cover exactly the same goods as the Sales Law, i.e. movable property. It can be noted, Madam President, that, among other things, property rights, securities and shares have been excluded from the Sales Law. It is clear from the preparatory work to the Sales Law that it is not appropriate to have the same cancellation rules for shares and right-of-occupancy shares as for other movable property and that the rules on remedying, i.e. any measure to restore the goods in operational condition after an error, cannot be applied to the purchase of movable property with certain rights. It goes without saying that the same reasoning should have been used in the case of live animals. The Consumer Sales Law states, as I said, that the general rule is that errors that appear within six months should be considered to have existed at the time of delivery. The exceptions are cases where this is incompatible with the nature of the goods or fault, which primarily focuses on the purchase of food. It should, in fact, be a natural conclusion that living animals can be compared to certain foods, which are also highly changing in nature. Food cannot be returned unchanged a few months after purchase, nor should live animals be possible in the same way as today. The same exception, Madam President, which applies to food should therefore also apply to live animals, when animals, like food, are undergoing changes, unlike more traditional consumer goods. Madam President, I would like Swedish horse breeding is well regarded in the world. Swedish-born horses are exported abroad, where they reap great successes, and breeding of horses is also one of the basic bolts in the Swedish horse industry. The breeding of horses also means a great deal to society at large in the form of everything from jobs to rural businesses and open landscapes. Swedish horse industry, especially the large horseback riding sector, consists mainly of us who use the horse for hobby activities or other recreational purposes. The problems with the current legislation mean that many horse breeders are currently highly hesitant about selling horses to private individuals. If the current trend continues, Swedish horse breeding will stop, and there is a risk that it will have a number of negative long-term effects, such as reduced rural entrepreneurship, loss of revenue for the industry and loss of jobs. Of course, Madam President, the consumer perspective is important. I myself am a consumer in this area. There must always be a balance between different interests. There are plenty of appropriate arrangements for new legislation. The most important thing is that the legislation is adapted to live animals. As it is today, all parties are harmed: the seller, the consumer and the animal. It has succeeded in reconciling the worst of two worlds. This must change, and it must change sooner rather than later.

i-d299eb6ffd2079c9-1192	Madam President, I would like The world is changing. Yes, every generation can claim that. But the fact is that globalisation, migration and digitalisation at many levels and at a rapid pace are shaping the society we know. Political philosophy, international politics and institutions and actors in democracy are changing. And a very uncertain change of tribe in the community contract shakes the happy street. Older sets of norms, values and laws for moral systems and trust are loosened and challenged by new, varied entrenchments of the common man. Old truths are challenged and replaced by new ones. The latter, Madam President, is particularly interesting in the light of the report we are dealing with today on media issues. Is it something that is a fundamental element of democratic society, the free and independent media that can report on cold facts and objective truths through honest journalistic work, which citizens should be able to rely on? This applies in particular to the scrutiny of power by the media. True and false is fundamental to account, just as in free and independent research. At the same time, there are few things that are now so much discussed as the situation of source-critical, impartial and factual media and journalistic work, and the very existence of that kind of media in the eyes of the public. What are the possibilities for individual citizens to access source-critical and objectively-oriented media? If we lift our eyes for a global analysis, we see a complex development. On the one hand, the flow of information has been democratised through social media and by the fact that more and more people can contribute to the common exchange and flow of information and news. On the other hand, the new technology enables propaganda and, let us say, one-way news to be pumped out and attached without reflecting on the meaning. This applies, of course, not least to the fact that people increasingly seek their information in their own filter bubbles or put more faith in Google searches than traditional media. This was also discussed in the Committee on Knowledge and Culture at the Nordic Council in Stockholm at the beginning of the week. This also takes the form of macro-level information and propaganda warfare. Not least, this is something that the Baltic States sense, as Russian channels, according to Baltic and Nordic representatives, have a very tendentious and angled broadcasting TV in order to influence the image of the Baltic Sea situation and Russia. Broadcasts can be distributed through companies elsewhere. It is extremely serious for trust in factual news reporting in our surrounding world and for the situation in the Baltic Sea region. In the polarized debate it is sometimes claimed that the Swedish democrats could be placed among those who want to circumscribe the freedom of the free media or have a different opinion on the value of public services. It's the other way around. Most recently in the committee's public hearing on public service, we praised the value of the Nordic public service companies. Whether other parties have been criticized for having tendencies of political impoverishment towards public services, I did not intend to touch on it today. Instead, I note that it is generally foolish to throw stones in glass houses and that, as we have previously proclaimed, we in Sweden democrats are critical of developments in those countries where the impartiality and independence of public services are challenged. Confidence in public service remains high but faltering, as more and more voices express criticism of lack of impartiality and objectivity. I can make a quick look. It was an article in Dagens Nyheter today about growing criticism of the balance of debate within the public service companies. Applications are launched that will give more perspectives on news. And voices in the media and among citizens have recently wondered whether public service is a body to review power or a body of power to examine the people. I think we can agree that it should be the former – at arm's length away from us in this House. Being in the public service and experiencing persistent criticism must lead to self-examination and self-criticism. Some tools that are close at hand are: to take the impression of how public service channels in our Nordic neighbours work, to broaden political reporting and debate with perspectives established among the public and to actively and integratedly work with programme ethics and independence to avoid just politicisation, polarisation and political unilaterality. Yes, it is urgent to identify the criticism in order to defend a fundamentally good idea and institution, as public service is. Madam President, I would like This week the Nordic Council held a session here in Stockholm. Nordic cooperation extends across many fields, including the media area. It is about the public service companies' collaboration, Nordvision, the Nordic film and television prize and so on. At the same time, there is the possibility of increased cooperation. This is something we in the Swedish Democrats express in our media movement. The aim is to strengthen the Nordic cultural community but also to learn from our Nordic neighbours how we can reflect societal developments, broaden the famous opinion corridor and reduce the risk of unilateral reporting. It could be a source for the public service broadcasters. They could receive constructive criticism and be able to learn how to improve. They could raise awareness of what perspectives and events they missed or avoided reporting on. Our hope is also that increased cooperation can have a positive effect on other media news reporting. This would strengthen the credibility of the entire media sector. In addition, series like the Bron and Skam have done more for the interest in the Nordic language community than various forms of political action have done in years. I support the reservations of the Swedish Democrats, Nos 1 and 2.

Madam President, I would like In addition to the motions included in the report, the Government's letter is also discussed in connection with the Swedish National Audit Office's review of Teracom Group AB's operations and the government's control of the company. The underlying objective of Teracom's activities is to ensure that the broadcasting of public service broadcasters can reach the entire population safely and efficiently, not least in view of a preparedness function. The Swedish National Audit Office notes that the company fulfils this objective fairly well but also strongly criticises other parts of Teracom's operations and the government's governance. It points to a number of side activities that have a weak or no link to the terrestrial networks and questions whether this new approach is compatible with the basic mission. According to the Swedish National Audit Office, the reorientation of Teracom means that it enters several competitive markets, and the risk of Teracom hampering private enterprise increases as a result. For large parts, there is currently no motive for state ownership," says Riksrevisionen. It also states that it is justified for the State to maintain terrestrial network activities but is clear that other activities, including telecommunications activities, do not need or should be owned by the State. For the terrestrial network business that lies in the company's basic assignment, it is not even considered that it is self-evident that the form of public limited liability company is the appropriate one. In other words, it is quite sharp criticism that the Swedish National Audit Office is in favour of the expansion of Teracom's operations and that the government has been inadequate in its governance of the company. Something surprising is the criticism rather casually dismissed by the government. It is believed that there is no reason whatsoever to review the state ownership of Teracom, nor any reason to review the organisational form. Madam President, I would like On behalf of the Alliance, we have written a joint special opinion in which we share the view that the high coverage requirements of terrestrial networks remain relevant for broadcasting programme services throughout the country and that, by extension, it is also a matter of functioning emergency preparedness in virtually any circumstances. But to dismiss the Swedish National Audit Office's criticisms as easily as the government does is not reasonable. The Alliance is of the simple opinion that where the state is needed, the state should be a good actor, but where the state is not needed, the tasks are better handled by others. In this way, we share in many respects the criticism that the Swedish National Audit Office makes and believe that the measures announced by the Government are inadequate. It is high time to review the role of the state in the market and thus also the ownership of Teracom. We also intend to come back to this in other contexts. We want to see an extended and deeper collaboration on news reporting between the Nordic public service companies. One model could be that the Swedish Radio Administration Foundation, Sweden's Television and Education Radio, in collaboration with the Nordic Council and the three broadcasters, will develop guidelines for how public service companies' news desks can annually conduct reciprocal exchanges of news reports with the other Nordic countries. In this way, the different countries can share their strengths and reduce the risk of unilateral reporting. Our hope is that this measure can have a positive impact on other media news reporting and thus strengthen the credibility of the entire media sector. This would further strengthen the credibility of independent news reporting, given the ongoing debate on fake news. As I have already mentioned, it is of the utmost importance that public service reporting is factual and accurate and that data is checked in the cases notified to the Radio and Television Audit Board. We propose that, at least in the case of public service news and fact-finding, there should be a social body that consistently checks the facts in the case of a notification. The Swedish Democrats also propose that a review be carried out of the broadcasting permits of the public service companies, to look at how broadcasting time can be set aside for a program of corrections. The programme can also be an opportunity for public services to report in more detail on a programme or report that has received many notifications but where it has been shown that the public has not been justified. Mr President, I would like to This debate concerns the report of the Committee on Justice on criminal matters. Annually, this committee deals with up to 150 requests for exercise from the general period of exercise to recriminalise acts or tighten the penalties for acts that exist in our criminal code. Criminal law and the criminal code are the backbone of justice policy. It is we legislators who bear responsibility for the fact that the judiciary has the tools to prosecute and impose sanctions. But criminal law is also restrictive. Not everything can be solved with punitive measures and neo-criminalisation. For example, criminal law does not answer the question of what makes some young people turn into habitual offenders, while others do not. Nor can criminal law itself solve structural societal challenges for us. If we want to stop crime in areas like Järva, we need to take in and understand what the research says. Criminological research indicates that developed criminal behaviour takes place in interactions between biological, psychological and social factors. Personality characteristics, concrete behavioral patterns such as hyperactivity, learning difficulties and emotional problems affect as much as parents' inability to care and a trusting and secure relationship. But antisocial development can also be influenced by the socio-economic structure. It is not obvious that growing up in a socio-economically vulnerable residential area leads to becoming a criminal. After all, the vast majority of children who grow up in Järva do not become criminals, and we should be clear about that. But what has been shown is that the social composition and structure of the residential area can affect the risk of developing crime, but in different ways for people with different individual conditions. And that's exactly what it looks like in Järva. The youths come from homes with prolonged stress, with parents constantly fighting, where piles of bills have not been paid, where there are too many living in the cramped apartment. In addition, there are children who have emotional problems or other learning difficulties. It is usually these young people who do not pass school and who stay out late in the evening. They are welcomed to the metro, and there is no question of their presence. That's where you're actually welcome. Since time passes, and suddenly you have learned the language of the street. It is being recruited into what is organised crime of drug trafficking, which then becomes gang dealings, control over the area and attacks on the rest of society, which tries to do something about insecurity. Mr President, I would like to If we really want to stop recruiting for crime in these areas, we must act both repressively and structurally. As I grew up, Plattan, as it was called, was the place most associated with drug trafficking, and it was there that I saw for the first time people who were in severe social vulnerability. Mr President, there is an enormous amount that we can do to prevent crime when planning our cities. From a prevention debate that has been very much about the perpetrator, we have over the years moved some of this focus to looking at the purely practical prevention, i.e. how we can build away the possibility of committing crimes. The opportunity makes the thief, it's usually called, and according to the research, it's actually in many ways just that. Dark pretenses, the lack of people who can see and, if necessary, sound alarms, poor lighting and other such things create opportunities to commit crimes for those who intend to do so. Since Sergel's square was rebuilt, security has increased significantly. Better lighting and removed corners are part, and another is to make sure that there is a stream of, in the absence of better words, ordinary people passing by. Flows of people with purposes other than committing crime reduce crime. When I talk to center parties around our municipalities, inventiveness is great in this area. For example, it is important to ensure that the number of different luminaires for street lighting in the municipality is as small as possible so that it is not unnecessarily difficult to produce spare parts when a lamp has broken. Another center party has told us about a place where there were molestations and robberies. There a new hundred yards were laid. Since its arrival, no such incidents had been reported. Another way to change the composition and increase the advantage of the security-creating majority is to plan for trade and entrepreneurship. I think that Ica in Sergel's square is of great importance because the whole place has become more secure – precisely for these reasons. Firstly, Mr President, it is our moral duty. i-d299eb6ffd2079c9-1409	Mr President, I would like to In a debate on penalties, however, we should remind ourselves of how criminal law works in Sweden. The penalty shall be related to the seriousness of the offence, while at the same time penalising similar offences. Looking back in history, no country has been able to deter people from committing crimes simply by using the punishment as a deterrent. In Sweden we do not lock people up because they can be dangerous but because a serious crime has been committed. Nor has the prison sentence been used in Sweden as a deterrent to not committing a crime, but to condemn the punishment that is appropriate to the crime that has been committed. It has proved time and again that imprisonment is not a deterrent, since some 60 percent of those in prison relapse into crime. The Prison Service believes that the purpose of a prison sentence is to give the client treatment and care and that the stay at the prison should be designed so that the person can best return to society. This might be good to remember. The belief that criminalisation and bans reduce crime is the closest thing to consensus in this Chamber. The investigation will review current legislation in order to see if more effective regulation for interception can be achieved while maintaining respect for privacy. i-d299eb6ffd2079c9-1601	Madam President, I would like There are some who call for Sweden to introduce anonymous witnesses, especially in times when we have many court hearings that are cancelled because witnesses do not dare to show up, and many unsolved crimes that are difficult to investigate. Then anonymous witnesses may feel like a good solution. But I think it's going to be a legally insecure future. The fact that people do not want to stand and testify is, according to the Crime Prevention Council, not just because they have been exposed to serious threats or violent crimes. The most common thing, according to a study that Brå has done, may be that you have a general fear of the process, which is not surprising, because a court hearing is a vulnerable situation. To enable effective law enforcement, therefore, Witnesses are required to stand and testify when required. That is why we have a public witness duty in Sweden and the court has the right to summon witnesses. However, there are threats to the person who is to testify, there is also protection, for example through various administrative measures such as the fact that one's address is not disclosed in the population register. In the court proceedings itself, it can be decided that a party or an audience may not be in the hall when witnesses or plaintiffs are to be heard. You can also hold the entire trial via video link. Anonymous witnesses are a real threat to the rule of law. In a witness hearing in a court of law, the court must make an assessment of the credibility of the witness, which is largely impossible if the witness is to be anonymous. The defendant will find it very difficult to defend himself against allegations made by an anonymous witness, and the examination of the witness's background, experience and personal circumstances will be made more difficult. Nor will it be the case that it is the witness who decides whether or not to remain anonymous, but it is the court that decides whether the threat is serious enough – a decision that can be appealed by the defendant. Thus, police and prosecutors could never guarantee that a witness can remain anonymous throughout the entire legal process. And what witness would stand if the anonymity could disappear at any time? Anonymous witnesses would pose a threat to the rule of law and cradle witnesses in the belief that they can unilaterally choose to remain anonymous. In reality, a majority of witnesses would be refused anonymity at a trial. On the other hand, we need to get more people to testify. Therefore, a better witness protection programme needs to be investigated, which informs prior to negotiation and also offers a more modern and better protection if the threat is too great. While we are talking about witnesses and what happens in Sweden's courts every day, I would also like to take this opportunity to pay tribute and thank all witness supporters who support witnesses in all Sweden's courts without payment every day. I thank you from the entire Committee on Justice. What heroes you are! Testimony in court is part of the police's duties, and police officers are called to a hearing according to the same rules as apply to all other witnesses. The judiciary is obliged to guarantee citizens legal certainty and efficiency in court proceedings. For various reasons, it sometimes takes quite a long time between preliminary investigation and the main hearing in a case. A policeman called a witness may therefore have taken parental leave, changed jobs or been at home for the care of a sick child once it is time for trial. The date of delivery of the testimony is also not adapted to the working hours or duties of the police. Many police officers, therefore, testify in court in their free time, even though the testimony is, in principle, exclusively a matter for the individual police. We in the Left Party believe that measures must be taken to make it easier for police officers to testify in court, while at the same time it must not lead to a loss of legal certainty in the form of suspension of proceedings or unjustified lengthy court proceedings. The government should therefore return to the Riksdag with proposals for measures to make it easier for police officers to testify in court. I request the approval of reservation 17.

The second major area where reforms are needed is to tear down all the walls that exist in order to achieve good cooperation between municipalities and county councils in terms of care and care for the very oldest. Good models are beginning to emerge for how this can happen. West Skaraborg, Ten Hundreds and Ängelholm are some examples. The problem is that there are many more areas where there are no good examples. Part of the explanation is that care for the elderly is also divided into a part governed by the Social Services Act and a part governed by the Health Care Act. One minute the nurse can make an immediate decision about an operation, based on the Health Care Act, but the next minute an official decision is required from an aid administrator. This is not how you can organize the modern elderly care of the future. It is time for us to have a modern care law based on the needs of the elderly and not after whether it is county councils or municipalities that are responsible. Although Swedish elderly care faces many and major challenges, there are no proposals from the left-wing government. Instead, it is concerned with making proposals that will seriously impair the care of the elderly, not improve it. What will have the greatest impact is undoubtedly the proposal to ban private companies in the care of the elderly, the Reepalus investigation. If it becomes a reality, a large number of private retirement homes and home care services will be closed – in nte because of their poor quality, not because the elderly and their relatives are dissatisfied, nor because the staff are dissatisfied, but only because the left-wing parties are distrusting private companies in the welfare sector. Swedish elderly care has enough challenges without well-functioning units having to be shut down for ideological reasons. Madam President, I would like Sweden needs new leadership, not least in order to be able to deal with the challenges facing elderly care.

In February, researchers at the Department of Social Work at Stockholm University published a new report on how the working conditions of elderly care have changed between 2005 and 2015. The study is based on questionnaire responses from approximately 1,200 nurses and health care assistants in the Swedish elderly care sector and from around 4,400 in corresponding positions in Denmark, Finland and Norway. The report shows several major changes in Swedish care for the elderly. There has been a significant increase in the number of helpers faced by a home worker during a working day, while the room for manoeuvre of the staff has been reduced. The contact with the managers doesn't work anymore, that's all. The staff of retirement homes experience their work as significantly more psychologically and physically heavy and also much more time-pressed in 2015 than in 2005. It's not moving forward. The Swedish model, which is characterised by joint and several responsibility, is the very basis for the welfare so many other countries would like to share with us. A protection system from the cradle to the grave gives the individual the opportunity, courage and strength to develop their full potential for the benefit of the whole of society, development and growth. Elderly care is a central area in this welfare building. The Social Affairs Committee's report 9 on older people is a motion report dealing with claims from the general exercise period 2015 and 2016. Madam President, I would like Coming as the last speaker means that much is likely to be repeated. The quality of care for the elderly not only affects the elderly themselves but, as has already been said, also their relatives. But let us not forget, too, that we are all slowly on the way to a hopefully well-deserved and eventful old age. The elderly are, therefore, something that affects everyone and should therefore be genuinely involved. In 2015, over 300 000 people had a home service or lived in a special housing facility for the elderly. Most often the debate is about the situation of the elderly and the quality of the different housing. However, at least as important is the issue of the quality of home care, as most elderly people are affected by this form of support. In addition to an additional 10 billion annually to the local government sector, for example to enable municipalities to localise their efforts in the care of the elderly, the Environmental Party, the Social Democrats and the Left Party in the budget for 2016–2018 have put in further investments of an additional 2 billion annually specifically at the care of the elderly in order to have more staff around the municipalities. More staff creates space to, for example, increase the quality of meals, give more time to outdoor stays and in different ways design care according to the needs and desires of the elderly. However, there are still shortcomings in care for the elderly, and there are major challenges for the future. Therefore, in 2015, the Government appointed a national investigator to develop proposals that can contribute to, and facilitate for, the municipalities to meet all these challenges. It is not just a question of increasing life expectancy, which we consider to be fundamentally very positive and desirable, leading to an increase in staffing needs in the care of the elderly and that competition for the workforce is tough when there is a shortage of labour in so many sectors of society. It is also about the organisation and content of the business. We need to strengthen the development towards an elderly care that is equal and safe and is characterized by increased participation and influence if both the users and the staff are to feel that the business is right and the one they have the right to expect. In order to support the municipalities, which are de facto those responsible for care for the elderly, the state can of course do more than simply provide financial resources. Here are some examples of measures taken by the government to contribute to further positive developments in the field of older people: The Government, in collaboration with the Swedish Municipalities and County Councils and the Association of Municipal Workers, has started work to strengthen the supply of skills in the care of the elderly. The aim is, among other things, to make it easier for those in care who have a lower level of education to become nurses. The Government has examined the conditions for a national quality plan for elderly care. The Swedish Institute for Standardisation has been given an increased mandate and funding to develop national standards for quality assurance of, among other things, elderly care. The Government has also commissioned the National Board of Health and Welfare to develop measures to develop primary care so that it is better adapted to the needs of the elderly. Violence against older women, which I think everyone has talked about, has also been highlighted, including through assignments to the National Board of Health and Welfare, assignments to the National Centre for Women's Peace and, not least, the work begun to establish an equality authority. This, Madam President, was just a few examples of action, and last week the national investigator, Susanne Rolfner Suvanto, came up with her proposals to ensure and improve the quality of care for the elderly. Madam President, I would like I call for the committee's proposal to be approved and for all reservations to be rejected.
